People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose I’m here because I haven’t quite been myself lately. A friend of mine from choir passed away very suddenly, and since then I’ve just been getting more upset over little things than I usually would.

I mean, I know everyone grieves, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but I’ll cry over something like the potluck lists, or get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve even started missing church planning things, which… I really never used to do. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you need to know.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Since my choir friend died. I kept thinking it would settle down after a week or two, but it’s still been happening. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not deliberately, anyway. I mean, I may not go out of my way toward certain reminders, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or places because of him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I usually enjoy… maybe I’m just a bit slower to get myself going sometimes. But I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease in enjoying them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble managing your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say most of the time, exactly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I’ll start crying over something silly, like the potluck lists, or I get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed a couple of church planning things I normally never would have missed. So… maybe I’m not managing it as well as I’d like to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a persistent sadness or emptiness inside that doesn't seem to go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness exactly… but I haven’t felt like myself since my choir friend passed so suddenly. I’ll cry over little things, like the potluck lists, and I get all worked up about phone calls that used to be easy for me. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… there’s definitely a sadness there, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in the choir or other activities you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I still care about choir, and I still want to be part of things. Maybe I’ve been a little slower to show up or get into it, but I wouldn’t say I’m less interested, exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still want to enjoy those things… I think it’s more that I’m not quite as quick to feel cheerful about them, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I do still care about the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't want to participate in activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I don’t want to participate. I still do want to be involved… maybe I’m just a bit less quick to jump in than I used to be. But it’s not that I’ve stopped wanting to do those things, if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you've lost pleasure in the things you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure, exactly. It’s maybe a little muted sometimes, but I can still enjoy things when I’m there. I still do want to be involved… maybe I’m just a bit less quick to jump in than I used to be. But it’s not that I’ve stopped wanting to do those things, if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you've lost pleasure in the things you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure, exactly. A friend of mine from choir passed away very suddenly, and since then I’ve just been getting more upset over little things than I usually would.\n\nI mean, I know everyone grieves, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but I’ll cry over something like the potluck lists, or get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve even started missing church planning things, which… I really never used to do. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you need to know.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month now, I think. Since my choir friend died. I kept thinking it would settle down after a week or two, but it’s still been happening. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not deliberately, anyway. I mean, I may not go out of my way toward certain reminders, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or places because of him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I usually enjoy… maybe I’m just a bit slower to get myself going sometimes. But I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease in enjoying them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble managing your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say most of the time, exactly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I’ll start crying over something silly, like the potluck lists, or I get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed a couple of church planning things I normally never would have missed. So… maybe I’m not managing it as well as I’d like to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a persistent sadness or emptiness inside that doesn't seem to go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness exactly… but I haven’t felt like myself since my choir friend passed so suddenly. I’ll cry over little things, like the potluck lists, and I get all worked up about phone calls that used to be easy for me. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… there’s definitely a sadness there, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in the choir or other activities you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I do still care about the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't want to participate in activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I don’t want to participate. I still want to enjoy those things… I think it’s more that I’m not quite as quick to feel cheerful about them, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I do still care about the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't want to participate in activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I don’t want to participate. I still do want to be involved… maybe I’m just a bit less quick to jump in than I used to be. But it’s not that I’ve stopped wanting to do those things, if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you've lost pleasure in the things you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure, exactly. It’s maybe a little muted sometimes, but I can still enjoy things when I’m there. I hope that’s answering it the right way… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things to deliberately avoid reminders of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been doing that on purpose, no. I mean, there are moments where something catches me off guard, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately avoiding reminders. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself going out of your way to avoid things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m going out of my way like that. Sometimes I might feel a little uneasy if something reminds me of her, but I haven’t noticed myself planning around it or avoiding places or anything. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding memories or thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate way. The memories and thoughts come up on their own, and I guess sometimes I don’t linger on them, but I wouldn’t say I’m trying to push them away. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away thoughts or memories of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really trying to push them away, no. I mean, sometimes if a memory comes up and it feels heavy, I might sort of move on to whatever I’m doing, but I’m not making a point of blocking it out. At least, I don’t think so… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been making an effort to stay away from things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been making a special effort to stay away from things. If something reminds me of her, it can catch me a little, but I’m not avoiding it on purpose. But I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease in enjoying them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble managing your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say most of the time, exactly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I’ll start crying over something silly, like the potluck lists, or I get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed a couple of church planning things I normally never would have missed. So… maybe I’m not managing it as well as I’d like to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a persistent sadness or emptiness inside that doesn't seem to go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness exactly… but I haven’t felt like myself since my choir friend passed so suddenly. I’ll cry over little things, like the potluck lists, and I get all worked up about phone calls that used to be easy for me. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… there’s definitely a sadness there, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in the choir or other activities you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I still care about choir, and I still want to be part of things. Maybe I’ve been a little slower to show up or get into it, but I wouldn’t say I’m less interested, exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still want to enjoy those things… I think it’s more that I’m not quite as quick to feel cheerful about them, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I do still care about the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't want to participate in activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I don’t want to participate. I still do want to be involved… maybe I’m just a bit less quick to jump in than I used to be. But it’s not that I’ve stopped wanting to do those things, if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you've lost pleasure in the things you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure, exactly. It’s maybe a little muted sometimes, but I can still enjoy things when I’m there. I hope that’s answering it the right way… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things to deliberately avoid reminders of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been doing that on purpose, no. I mean, there are moments where something catches me off guard, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately avoiding reminders. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself going out of your way to avoid things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m going out of my way like that. Sometimes I might feel a little uneasy if something reminds me of her, but I haven’t noticed myself planning around it or avoiding places or anything. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding memories or thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate way. The memories and thoughts come up on their own, and I guess sometimes I don’t linger on them, but I wouldn’t say I’m trying to push them away. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away thoughts or memories of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really trying to push them away, no. I mean, sometimes if a memory comes up and it feels heavy, I might sort of move on to whatever I’m doing, but I’m not making a point of blocking it out. At least, I don’t think so… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been making an effort to stay away from things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been making a special effort to stay away from things. If something reminds me of her, it can catch me a little, but I’m not avoiding it on purpose. I hope that’s clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things because they remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do, no… I mean, I still go where I need to go and see people. If something reminds me of her, I might feel it for a moment, but I’m not really avoiding people or places because of it. I hope that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I haven’t quite been myself lately. A friend of mine from choir passed away very suddenly, and since then I’ve just been getting more upset over little things than I usually would.\n\nI mean, I know everyone grieves, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but I’ll cry over something like the potluck lists, or get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve even started missing church planning things, which… I really never used to do. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you need to know.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month now, I think. Since my choir friend died. I kept thinking it would settle down after a week or two, but it’s still been happening. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not deliberately, anyway. I mean, I may not go out of my way toward certain reminders, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or places because of him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I usually enjoy… maybe I’m just a bit slower to get myself going sometimes. But I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease in enjoying them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble managing your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say most of the time, exactly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I’ll start crying over something silly, like the potluck lists, or I get all worked up about phone calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed a couple of church planning things I normally never would have missed. So… maybe I’m not managing it as well as I’d like to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a persistent sadness or emptiness inside that doesn't seem to go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness exactly… but I haven’t felt like myself since my choir friend passed so suddenly. I’ll cry over little things, like the potluck lists, and I get all worked up about phone calls that used to be easy for me. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… there’s definitely a sadness there, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in the choir or other activities you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I still care about choir, and I still want to be part of things. Maybe I’ve been a little slower to show up or get into it, but I wouldn’t say I’m less interested, exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still want to enjoy those things… I think it’s more that I’m not quite as quick to feel cheerful about them, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I do still care about the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't want to participate in activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I don’t want to participate. I still do want to be involved… maybe I’m just a bit less quick to jump in than I used to be. But it’s not that I’ve stopped wanting to do those things, if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you've lost pleasure in the things you usually enjoy since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure, exactly. It’s maybe a little muted sometimes, but I can still enjoy things when I’m there. I hope that’s answering it the right way… I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things to deliberately avoid reminders of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been doing that on purpose, no. I mean, there are moments where something catches me off guard, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately avoiding reminders. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself going out of your way to avoid things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m going out of my way like that. Sometimes I might feel a little uneasy if something reminds me of her, but I haven’t noticed myself planning around it or avoiding places or anything. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding memories or thoughts of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate way. The memories and thoughts come up on their own, and I guess sometimes I don’t linger on them, but I wouldn’t say I’m trying to push them away. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away thoughts or memories of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really trying to push them away, no. I mean, sometimes if a memory comes up and it feels heavy, I might sort of move on to whatever I’m doing, but I’m not making a point of blocking it out. At least, I don’t think so… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been making an effort to stay away from things that remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been making a special effort to stay away from things. If something reminds me of her, it can catch me a little, but I’m not avoiding it on purpose. I hope that’s clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things because they remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do, no… I mean, I still go where I need to go and see people. If something reminds me of her, I might feel it for a moment, but I’m not really avoiding people or places because of it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things, like places, people, or activities, that you've been avoiding because they remind you of your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really think of anything specific like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wishing you could see or talk to your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that… I suppose anyone would wish that sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m sitting around wishing it often. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong longing to be with your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a strong longing, exactly. I do miss her, and there are moments where I think, “I wish I could tell her this,” but I try not to sit with that too long. So… maybe a little sometimes, but not in a way that feels constant. Is that an okay way to put it?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the sadness you're experiencing is overwhelming and affecting most areas of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I have had to let other church members take over calls, lists, and parts of the luncheon because I am not keeping up the way I always did before.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming in every area, exactly… I’m still trying to do what I’m supposed to. But I have noticed I’m not keeping up like I used to, especially with church things. I’ve had to let other members take over some of the calls and lists, and parts of the luncheon, because I just wasn’t staying on top of it the way I always did before. I feel a little bad saying that, but… that’s probably the main way it’s been affecting me.
